[Director 指令] **[Patch] 叙事汇聚：regularization mechanism 显式化**

Reviewer 指出论文最大叙事损失：三个 null results（scrambled≈correct, frozen probing near-parity, mean pooling≈BiGRU）共同指向"classification DAPT 通过正则化而非语义学习提供优势"，但论文从未显式串联这条链。

**三处修改（总改动量 word-neutral，不能增加页数）：**

### 1. Abstract 最后一句（abstract.tex L9）
当前："These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection."

改为更具体提及 regularization：大意是 classification adaptation 通过优化正则化（而非语义特征学习）提供跨条件鲁棒性。保持一句话，不加长。

### 2. Introduction C3（introduction.tex）
当前 C3 是杂货清单（variance reversal + mixed-LLM + interpretability + 266× speedup）。重构为统一主线：

**核心 pitch**："Mechanistic insight: classification DAPT works through optimization regularization, not semantic feature learning — evidenced by frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1pp), label content irrelevance (d=0.09), and temporal modeling irrelevance (|Δ|<1pp)."

266× speedup 和 interpretability 可以保留为子点，但主线必须是 regularization insight。

### 3. Discussion（conclusion.tex）
在 "Variance reversal on human data" 段落之前或之后，加 2-3 句话汇聚：

"Three null results converge on a single mechanistic interpretation: frozen probing shows classification and MLM DAPT produce near-equivalent representations (AA gap 3.1pp; Appendix~\ref{app:frozen_verify}), scrambled labels match correct labels (d=0.09), and mean pooling matches BiGRU (|Δ|<1pp). Classification supervision thus improves detection not through better per-turn representations or label semantics, but through optimization regularization that stabilizes downstream training — an effect that becomes critical under distribution pressure (mixed-LLM collapse; Section~\ref{sec:mixed_llm})."

**硬约束**：
- 不要动 experiments.tex
- 不要动 references.bib
- 总字数 word-neutral（压缩其他文字腾空间）

  - conclusion.tex 的 Limitations 段落（已压缩为 3 段）

```text
1	% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97). Classification loss may regularize representations independently of label content, paralleling self-supervised methods where pretraining structure outweighs label accuracy~\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}.
8	
9	\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}
10	ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.
11	
12	\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}
13	Classification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\,=\,0.087 vs.\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\ref{app:frozen_verify}).
14	
15	\paragraph{Encoder capacity, temporal, and lexical analysis.}
16	Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp), confirming order-invariant detection. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 0.961 on DD).
17	
18	\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}
19	OOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\% (Appendix~\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.
20	
21	\section{Conclusion}
22	
23	Classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness ($n$\,=\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{app:efficiency}).
24	
25	\section{Limitations}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SBHBJfhpg3xypqj4E1Gzjf`</summary>

```text
1	% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	\begin{abstract}
3	Detecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.
4	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families: Llama Guard~3, a widely deployed safety classifier, detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation.
5	We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.
6	On single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818 vs.\ 0.291 without adaptation).
7	Across three architectures (125--355M), the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with scale, though architecture and pretraining differences prevent isolating scale as the sole factor.
8	Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic evaluations (observed across 3 seeds), with limited sensitivity to label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.
9	These findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.
10	\end{abstract}
11

_source line 2573 · 2026-05-21T14:06:31.506Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019WqW6zhaqF532yB6NnXVyn`</summary>

```text
1	% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	
5	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
6	
7	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
8	
9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
10	
11	Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
16	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
17	\label{fig:architecture}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying the most robust adaptation and three counter-intuitive findings.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data shows classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (descriptive evidence at $n$=3 seeds), temporal order is irrelevant ($\Delta < 1$\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Architecture- and scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data (3 seeds), but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}). The 9-class taxonomy serves as interpretability tool and ablation infrastructure (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}). Continued pretraining is 266$\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge and near-saturates at $N$=50 (AA\,=\,0.969; Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}).
29	\end{itemize}
30	
```

    "originalFile": "% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\\begin{abstract}\nDetecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families: Llama Guard~3, a widely deployed safety classifier, detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation.\nWe isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.\nOn single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818 vs.\\ 0.291 without adaptation).\nAcross three architectures (125--355M), the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with scale, though architecture and pretraining differences prevent isolating scale as the sole factor.\nClassification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic evaluations (observed across 3 seeds), with limited sensitivity to label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.\nThese findings show that the adaptation mechanism, not just domain exposure, determines cross-condition robustness in multi-turn jailbreak detection.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying the most robust adaptation and three counter-intuitive findings.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data shows classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (descriptive evidence at $n$=3 seeds), temporal order is irrelevant ($\\Delta < 1$\\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Architecture- and scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale.     "oldString": "Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). Classification loss may regularize representations independently of label content, paralleling self-supervised methods where pretraining structure outweighs label accuracy~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}.",
    "newString": "Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97). Classification loss may regularize representations independently of label content, paralleling self-supervised methods where pretraining structure outweighs label accuracy~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp), confirming order-invariant detection. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness ($n$\\,=\\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

Classification loss may regularize representations independently of label content, paralleling self-supervised methods where pretraining structure outweighs label accuracy~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}.",

    "newString": "\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp), confirming order-invariant detection. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness ($n$\\,=\\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

Negative results on label correctness ($n$\\,=\\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}), label irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}, not semantic feature learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness ($n$\\,=\\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms.     "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), label irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}, not semantic feature learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness ($n$\\,=\\,3), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. **用户消息：** [Director 指令] [Patch] 论文审计发现 3 个 critical 和若干重要 warning，DDL 2026-05-25，必须立即修复。目标 EMNLP 2026 ARR。这次不用验证引用。

## Critical 修复

### C1: 符号冲突 $d$
- method.tex:14 定义 $d$ 为 embedding 维度（$h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$）
- experiments.tex 多处用 $d$ 表示 Cohen's effect size（$d < 0.1$, $d = 0.44$）
- **修复**：将 method.tex 中的 embedding 维度 $d$ 改为 $D$ 或 $d_h$，全文统一

### C2: "1.8%" 数字来源不清
- abstract/intro/experiments 三处声称 "Llama Guard 3 detects only 1.8%"
- 但引用的 Table 只显示 DD F1=0.025, AA F1=0.000
- **修复**：添加脚注或括号说明 1.8% 的计算方法（如 "pooled recall across both attack families"），或修正数字使之与表格一致

### C3: Benign-side 贡献无对应实验子节
- Contribution 3 在 Intro 明确声称 benign-side adaptation 数字（FPR 58-68% → 0-1.5%）
- 但 Experiments section 无任何子节报告 benign-side 实验，数据仅在 Discussion 一句话和 Appendix
- **修复**：在 Experiments section 增加一个简短子节（如 4.8 Benign Distribution Shift）报告 benign adaptation 核心结果，引用 Appendix 的完整表格。不需要长——3-5 句 + 引用 appendix table 即可。注意 8 页限制。

## Warning 修复

### W1: 去 AI 写作标志 — em-dash
- 正文 6 处、appendix 15+ 处使用 LaTeX em-dash (---)
- **全部替换**为逗号、括号、分号或拆分为独立句子
- 这是审稿人识别 AI 写作最显著的标志

### W2: 去 AI 写作标志 — -ing 分词结尾
- experiments.tex:70 "exposing a difficulty threshold that..."
- experiments.tex:74 "demonstrating that classification supervision stabilizes..."  
- experiments.tex:140 "confirming that detection relies on..."
- **改为独立句子**

### W3: Evidence hierarchy 术语限定
- experiments.tex:6 Terminology 段将 evidence hierarchy 定义为固定顺序 "vanilla < MLM < classification"
- 但 Section 5.5 ToxicChat 结果证明该顺序在 human data 上反转
- **修复**：Terminology 段加限定词 "on synthetic data"

### W4: 内部文档追踪 ID 删除
- 所有主要 .tex 文件首行 LaTeX 注释暴露内部 ID：
  - abstract.tex "D073 Phase 2 revision"
  - related_work.tex "D048 deep writing optimization"
  - experiments.tex "D024 compressed"
  - method.tex "D024 compressed"
  - conclusion.tex "D073 Phase 2 revision"
  - introduction.tex "expanded for 8-page target"
- **全部删除或替换为通用注释**（ARR 提交可能包含 .tex 源码）

### W5: Contribution 1 "thirteen configurations at three scales"
- Introduction Contribution 1 声称 "thirteen encoder configurations at three scales"
- 但 Method section 只描述 7 个 DeBERTa-v3-base 变体，RoBERTa 变体到 Experiments 4.3 才出现
- **修复**：在 Method 末尾增加一句说明 cross-architecture extension 设计意图

## 优先级
C1 > C3 > C2 > W1 > W2 > W4 > W3 > W5

修复后编译确认页数仍在 8 页内。

Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.
6	
7	Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}
8	
9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
10	
11	Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
16	\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
17	\label{fig:architecture}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).
21	
22	Our contributions:
23	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
24	  \item \textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying the most robust adaptation and three counter-intuitive findings.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data shows classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (descriptive evidence at $n$=3 seeds), temporal order is irrelevant ($\Delta < 1$\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.
25	
26	  \item \textbf{Architecture- and scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
27	
28	  \item \textbf{Mechanistic interpretation: optimization regularization, not semantic learning.} Three null results converge: frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\,pp), label content irrelevance ($d$\,=\,0.09), and temporal modeling irrelevance ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp) indicate classification supervision provides robustness through optimization regularization that stabilizes downstream training. This benefit is condition-dependent (variance reversal on human data) but classification DAPT remains strongest under mixed-LLM training (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}). Continued pretraining is 266$\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{app:data_efficiency}).
29	\end{itemize}
30	
```

```text
1	% method.tex — D024 compressed
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	Cross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}).
7	
8	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
9	
10	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
11	
12	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
13	
14	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\small
19	\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}
20	\label{tab:variants}
21	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
22	\toprule
23	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Objective} & \textbf{Tests whether\ldots} \\
24	\midrule
25	9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\
26	Binary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\
27	Scrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\
28	MLM-only & 15\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\
29	Topic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\
30	Wiki MLM & 15\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\
31	Vanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	The \textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\text{strategy}} + \alpha \cdot L_{\text{intent}}$ ($\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\}$; all values yield F1\,=\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78.
37	
38	The \textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.
39	The \textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns, preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.
40	The \textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.
41	The \textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.
42	The \textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.
43	The \textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.
44	
45	\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}
46	
47	Turn embeddings $\{h_1, \ldots, h_K\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\bar{h} = [\overrightarrow{h_K};\; \overleftarrow{h_1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{512}$.
48	A two-layer MLP ($512 \to 256 \to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\bar{h}$ to $\hat{y} \in \{0, 1\}$.
49	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.
50	This frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.
51	Three-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
52	
```
