    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "Three-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).",
    "newString": "Three-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\nFor cross-architecture validation, we replicate the vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base and RoBERTa-large (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}), yielding thirteen total configurations across three encoder scales.",
    "originalFile": "% method.tex\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nCross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}).\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^D$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns, preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{512}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($512 \\to 256 \\to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nThree-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n",

  "new_string": "The 9-class taxonomy provides interpretable characterization of known attacks ($\\kappa$=0.78, $n$=50; Appendix~\\ref{sec:strategy_full}) but degrades to near-random on OOD ($\\kappa$=0.126; Appendix~\\ref{app:strategy_pred}). Its value is twofold: as ablation infrastructure (scrambled labels produce equivalent detection) and as an interpretability tool for known attacks; detection operates through domain-adapted \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Benign Distribution Shift}\n\\label{sec:benign_shift}\n\nAll preceding OOD results use IID benign data; attack-side generalization does not imply deployment readiness.     "oldString": "\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nBenign distribution shift, not attack-side generalization, is the primary deployment obstacle (Section~\\ref{sec:benign_shift}).",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), label irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}, not semantic feature learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders, providing robustness through optimization regularization rather than semantic learning; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

```text
657

Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.
Benign distribution shift, not attack-side generalization, is the primary deployment obstacle (Section 4.9).

658

6

659

663

Classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders, providing robustness
through optimization regularization rather than semantic learning; continued pretraining is 266×
faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix U).

664

7

665

Multi-turn human validation. All multi-turn
findings are derived from synthetic conversations
generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18–
42 pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multiturn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether
the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored
multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.

654

700

fine-tuning closes this gap to ≤5.8 pp on ActorAttack (F1 = 0.997±0.005 vs. 0.939±0.047 frozen;
Table 17; Appendix R).
Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer. Scale
validation (1,000 conversations; Appendix J) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40–
56 pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows
jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800–1.000),
but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370–0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix H). The perturbation
evaluation (n=76) remains underpowered.

Conclusion

Limitations

Strategy labels. Persuasion strategy labels
(n=50 human validation, κ=0.78) are produced
by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small
per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD
strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7% (κ=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families.
We plan to release annotation prompts and data for
independent verification.

Turn-count distribution. Benign conversations
contain 12–20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6–10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3–8; the three categories have
non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations
argue against length as the primary detection cue:
(a) early detection at equal-length prefixes K=1
and K=2 achieves AA OOD F1 = 0.773 and 0.935
(Appendix 8); (b) vanilla models access the same
length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall;
and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns
yields FPR = 0% (Appendix T).
Temporal aggregation dilution. When subtle
attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes
attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix T); in practice, adversaries would not dilute
their own successful attacks.
OOD benign distribution. OOD results reflect
attack-side generalization only (Section 5). Even
with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE = 0.126–0.141; Appendix Q)
suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.
Frozen encoder assumption. The frozen Stage-1
design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end
9

721

Detection architecture. Binary vs. 9-class equivalence (F1 gap ≤3.9 pp) suggests the binary head
is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1 ≥ 0.990),
serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not
challenge any variant.

722

Encoder scale. Whether findings hold at LLMscale encoders (7B+) remains open; samearchitecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.
DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity
effect to scale alone.

728

Scope and single-turn transfer. Only
persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall
within scope; single-turn token-level attacks (Zou
et al., 2023), non-persuasive strategies, and
non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat
performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer;
doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat
macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341±0.004 while
multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the
architecture targets multi-turn detection.

Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks.     "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "For cross-architecture validation, we replicate the vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base and RoBERTa-large (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}), yielding thirteen total configurations across three encoder scales.",
    "newString": "Cross-architecture validation on RoBERTa-base and -large (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) yields thirteen configurations across three scales.",
    "originalFile": "% method.tex\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nCross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2). Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}).\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^D$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns, preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{512}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($512 \\to 256 \\to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nThree-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\nFor cross-architecture validation, we replicate the vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base and RoBERTa-large (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}), yielding thirteen total configurations across three encoder scales.\n",

    "newString": "Classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization, not semantic learning, and is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge.",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses; MLM may capture LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), label irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}, not semantic feature learning.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders, providing robustness through optimization regularization rather than semantic learning; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

389	
390	\paragraph{Post-hoc calibration.}
391	ROC AUC\,=\,0.700$\pm$0.054 indicates insufficient discrimination between WildChat benign and jailbreak conversations at the representation level. At TPR$\geq$95\%, the optimal threshold still yields FPR\,=\,62.7\%---far above deployable levels. Temperature scaling cannot improve this trade-off, as ROC curves are invariant to monotonic score transforms. The AUC ceiling reflects genuine domain shift: WildChat benign and jailbreak representations overlap substantially, and no post-hoc calibration can overcome this; in-domain benign adaptation is required.
392	
393	\section{BERT-base Proxy Baseline}

571	\end{table}
572	
573	Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} compares inference cost. On matched data (IID test), DeBERTa+BiGRU processes a conversation in 12.1\,ms---28$\times$ slower than TF-IDF (0.43\,ms) but this speed gap purchases +71\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.972 vs.\ 0.261; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}). LLM-as-judge approaches are orders of magnitude slower ($\sim$5.2\,s per conversation for Qwen3-8B 3-shot) while achieving substantially lower OOD detection (DD\,=\,0.566, AA\,=\,0.652; Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). Zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves F1\,=\,0.0 on DD OOD because it predicts all conversations as benign (Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). The full DeBERTa+BiGRU pipeline requires 862\,MB GPU memory, enabling deployment on consumer hardware.
574	
575	\section{Data Efficiency}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NwfzDZhgDbzjQwnVubqZmo`</summary>

```text
453	Table~\ref{tab:frozen_verify} compares frozen probing across DAPT objectives and against end-to-end fine-tuning. On easy OOD (DD, FITD), frozen MLM and frozen 9-class representations perform comparably ($\Delta \leq 1.3$\,pp). On hard OOD (ActorAttack), 9-class retains a modest advantage (0.972 vs.\ 0.941, $\Delta$\,=\,3.1\,pp) with lower variance ($\sigma$\,=\,0.026 vs.\ 0.037). Both far exceed vanilla encoders ($\leq$0.318; Table~\ref{tab:bert_proxy}). The E2E advantage over frozen 9-class is $\leq$2.5\,pp, confirming that Stage-1 representation quality is the primary source of OOD generalization.
454	
455	A striking asymmetry emerges when comparing frozen probing to the full pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}): MLM frozen representations \emph{outperform} the full MLM pipeline on AA OOD (0.941 vs.\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen exactly matches its full pipeline (0.972 vs.\ 0.972). This indicates that MLM's cross-seed instability originates in Stage-2 BiGRU training rather than Stage-1 DAPT---the MLM encoder produces adequate representations that are then degraded by unstable downstream optimization. Classification DAPT, by contrast, produces representations that remain robust through Stage-2 training, explaining its consistently lower variance (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
456	
457	This two-level structure---DAPT vs.\ no-DAPT producing a representation quality leap (+70\,pp), classification vs.\ MLM DAPT producing a stability difference---is consistent with two further observations: $\lambda$=0 matches $\lambda > 0$ during E2E fine-tuning (Table~\ref{tab:lambda}), and mean pooling matches BiGRU under E2E (Table~\ref{tab:temporal}). Once DAPT representations are strong, downstream configuration details have minimal impact.
458	
459	\section{Temporal Aggregation Comparison}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Wdtp8dDpXKNnrW791Vy22z`</summary>

```text
505	\paragraph{Length confound.}
506	Benign conversations (12--20 turns) and jailbreak conversations (3--10 turns) have zero length overlap in the OOD evaluation sets, raising the concern that the model discriminates by conversation length rather than content. Three observations argue against this interpretation:
507	(1)~Vanilla DeBERTa has access to identical length information via the BiGRU but achieves recall $\leq$2.6\% across all bins---length alone is insufficient without domain-adapted representations.
508	(2)~At prefix $K$=1, where all conversations are length-equalized, 9-class still achieves F1\,=\,0.773$\pm$0.041 on ActorAttack (Table~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}), demonstrating content-based detection independent of length.
509	(3)~The model is trained on IID data (Crescendo) and cannot learn OOD-specific length distributions; generalization to unseen length patterns must rely on content features that transfer across attack families.
```

621	\end{table}
622	
623	During Stage-2 E2E fine-tuning, the auxiliary persuasion loss weight $\lambda$ has limited impact on detection (Table~\ref{tab:lambda}): the maximum AA OOD gap across all $\lambda$ values is 5.6\,pp (single seed), comparable to the cross-seed variance ($\sim$6\,pp) observed in the main results. All configurations converge to perfect validation F1 within epoch 1, indicating that DAPT representations already encode sufficient discriminative features for downstream classification (Section~\ref{app:frozen_verify}). The value of persuasion strategy classification lies in Stage-1 DAPT---where it shapes encoder representations that transfer across attack families---not in the Stage-2 auxiliary loss.
624	
625	\section{OOD Strategy Prediction Analysis}

```text
662	\end{table}
663	
664	Table~\ref{tab:ensemble} shows that majority vote never corrupts correct predictions (Broke\,=\,0 for all variants). MLM benefits most from ensembling (+8.6\,pp DD, +6.0\,pp AA), because its per-seed errors are complementary---each seed fails on different samples, and the majority corrects these. This further exposes MLM's seed instability: its individual seeds are unreliable, but their errors are uncorrelated. By contrast, 9-class seeds are already highly consistent (std\,$\leq$\,0.026), leaving minimal room for ensemble improvement (+0.3--0.9\,pp). Vanilla errors are systematic (all seeds fail on the same OOD samples), so ensembling provides no benefit.
665	
666	\section{Seed-Level F1 Comparisons}

        "lines": [
          " Table~\\ref{tab:wildchat_fpr} evaluates false positive rates on 226 WildChat benign multi-turn conversations from real GPT-3.5 users (avg 7.4 turns). All variants produce FPR far exceeding deployable thresholds: 9-class is worst at 68.1\\%, followed by vanilla (48.1\\%) and MLM (44.5\\%). Domain adaptation \\emph{increases} FPR relative to vanilla (+20\\,pp for 9-class), indicating that continued pretraining amplifies dependence on the training benign distribution. This is consistent with the cross-LLM false positive spike (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_llm_error}): models trained on synthetic Qwen3-8B benign text implicitly treat unfamiliar conversational styles as jailbreak indicators. High MLM seed variance (std\\,=\\,14.3\\%) suggests that unsupervised pretraining is particularly unstable under benign distribution shift. Deployment requires per-domain benign calibration or mixed-source benign training data.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Post-hoc calibration.}",
          "-ROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700$\\pm$0.054 indicates insufficient discrimination between WildChat benign and jailbreak conversations at the representation level. At TPR$\\geq$95\\%, the optimal threshold still yields FPR\\,=\\,62.7\\%---far above deployable levels. Temperature scaling cannot improve this trade-off, as ROC curves are invariant to monotonic score transforms. The AUC ceiling reflects genuine domain shift: WildChat benign and jailbreak representations overlap substantially, and no post-hoc calibration can overcome this; in-domain benign adaptation is required.",
          "+ROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700$\\pm$0.054 indicates insufficient discrimination between WildChat benign and jailbreak conversations at the representation level. At TPR$\\geq$95\\%, the optimal threshold still yields FPR\\,=\\,62.7\\%, far above deployable levels. Temperature scaling cannot improve this trade-off, as ROC curves are invariant to monotonic score transforms. The AUC ceiling reflects genuine domain shift: WildChat benign and jailbreak representations overlap substantially, and no post-hoc calibration can overcome this; in-domain benign adaptation is required.",
          " ",
          " \\section{BERT-base Proxy Baseline}",
          " \\label{app:bert_proxy}"

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:frozen_verify} compares frozen probing across DAPT objectives and against end-to-end fine-tuning. On easy OOD (DD, FITD), frozen MLM and frozen 9-class representations perform comparably ($\\Delta \\leq 1.3$\\,pp). On hard OOD (ActorAttack), 9-class retains a modest advantage (0.972 vs.\\ 0.941, $\\Delta$\\,=\\,3.1\\,pp) with lower variance ($\\sigma$\\,=\\,0.026 vs.\\ 0.037). Both far exceed vanilla encoders ($\\leq$0.318; Table~\\ref{tab:bert_proxy}). The E2E advantage over frozen 9-class is $\\leq$2.5\\,pp, confirming that Stage-1 representation quality is the primary source of OOD generalization.",
          " ",
          "-A striking asymmetry emerges when comparing frozen probing to the full pipeline (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}): MLM frozen representations \\emph{outperform} the full MLM pipeline on AA OOD (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen exactly matches its full pipeline (0.972 vs.\\ 0.972). This indicates that MLM's cross-seed instability originates in Stage-2 BiGRU training rather than Stage-1 DAPT---the MLM encoder produces adequate representations that are then degraded by unstable downstream optimization. Classification DAPT, by contrast, produces representations that remain robust through Stage-2 training, explaining its consistently lower variance (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).",
          "+A striking asymmetry emerges when comparing frozen probing to the full pipeline (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}): MLM frozen representations \\emph{outperform} the full MLM pipeline on AA OOD (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen exactly matches its full pipeline (0.972 vs.\\ 0.972). This indicates that MLM's cross-seed instability originates in Stage-2 BiGRU training rather than Stage-1 DAPT: the MLM encoder produces adequate representations that are then degraded by unstable downstream optimization. Classification DAPT, by contrast, produces representations that remain robust through Stage-2 training, explaining its consistently lower variance (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).",
          " ",
          " This two-level structure---DAPT vs.\\ no-DAPT producing a representation quality leap (+70\\,pp), classification vs.\\ MLM DAPT producing a stability difference---is consistent with two further observations: $\\lambda$=0 matches $\\lambda > 0$ during E2E fine-tuning (Table~\\ref{tab:lambda}), and mean pooling matches BiGRU under E2E (Table~\\ref{tab:temporal}). Once DAPT representations are strong, downstream configuration details have minimal impact.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          " \\paragraph{Length confound.}",
          " Benign conversations (12--20 turns) and jailbreak conversations (3--10 turns) have zero length overlap in the OOD evaluation sets, raising the concern that the model discriminates by conversation length rather than content. Three observations argue against this interpretation:",
          "-(1)~Vanilla DeBERTa has access to identical length information via the BiGRU but achieves recall $\\leq$2.6\\% across all bins---length alone is insufficient without domain-adapted representations.",
          "+(1)~Vanilla DeBERTa has access to identical length information via the BiGRU but achieves recall $\\leq$2.6\\% across all bins; length alone is insufficient without domain-adapted representations.",
          " (2)~At prefix $K$=1, where all conversations are length-equalized, 9-class still achieves F1\\,=\\,0.773$\\pm$0.041 on ActorAttack (Table~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}), demonstrating content-based detection independent of length.",
          " (3)~The model is trained on IID data (Crescendo) and cannot learn OOD-specific length distributions; generalization to unseen length patterns must rely on content features that transfer across attack families.",
          " "
        ]
      }

571	\end{table}

573	Table~\ref{tab:efficiency} compares inference cost. On matched data (IID test), DeBERTa+BiGRU processes a conversation in 12.1\,ms (28$\times$ slower than TF-IDF (0.43\,ms) but this speed gap purchases +71\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\,=\,0.972 vs.\ 0.261; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}). LLM-as-judge approaches are orders of magnitude slower ($\sim$5.2\,s per conversation for Qwen3-8B 3-shot) while achieving substantially lower OOD detection (DD\,=\,0.566, AA\,=\,0.652; Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). Zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves F1\,=\,0.0 on DD OOD because it predicts all conversations as benign (Table~\ref{tab:llm_judge}). The full DeBERTa+BiGRU pipeline requires 862\,MB GPU memory, enabling deployment on consumer hardware.
574

Zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves F1\\,=\\,0.0 on DD OOD because it predicts all conversations as benign (Table~\\ref{tab:llm_judge}). The full DeBERTa+BiGRU pipeline requires 862\\,MB GPU memory, enabling deployment on consumer hardware.",
          "+Table~\\ref{tab:efficiency} compares inference cost. On matched data (IID test), DeBERTa+BiGRU processes a conversation in 12.1\\,ms, 28$\\times$ slower than TF-IDF (0.43\\,ms), but this speed gap purchases +71\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.972 vs.\\ 0.261; Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}). LLM-as-judge approaches are orders of magnitude slower ($\\sim$5.2\\,s per conversation for Qwen3-8B 3-shot) while achieving substantially lower OOD detection (DD\\,=\\,0.566, AA\\,=\\,0.652; Table~\\ref{tab:llm_judge}). Zero-shot Qwen3-8B achieves F1\\,=\\,0.0 on DD OOD because it predicts all conversations as benign (Table~\\ref{tab:llm_judge}). The full DeBERTa+BiGRU pipeline requires 862\\,MB GPU memory, enabling deployment on consumer hardware.",
          " ",
          " \\section{Data Efficiency}",
          " \\label{app:data_efficiency}"
        ]

          "-Table~\\ref{tab:ensemble} shows that majority vote never corrupts correct predictions (Broke\\,=\\,0 for all variants). MLM benefits most from ensembling (+8.6\\,pp DD, +6.0\\,pp AA), because its per-seed errors are complementary---each seed fails on different samples, and the majority corrects these. 621	\end{table}
622	
623	During Stage-2 E2E fine-tuning, the auxiliary persuasion loss weight $\lambda$ has limited impact on detection (Table~\ref{tab:lambda}): the maximum AA OOD gap across all $\lambda$ values is 5.6\,pp (single seed), comparable to the cross-seed variance ($\sim$6\,pp) observed in the main results. All configurations converge to perfect validation F1 within epoch 1, indicating that DAPT representations already encode sufficient discriminative features for downstream classification (Section~\ref{app:frozen_verify}). The value of persuasion strategy classification lies in Stage-1 DAPT---where it shapes encoder representations that transfer across attack families---not in the Stage-2 auxiliary loss.
624	
625	\section{OOD Strategy Prediction Analysis}
